Between 1980 and 1985, nine [offensives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panjshir_offensives "Panjshir offensives") were launched into the strategically important [Panjshir Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panjshir_Valley "Panjshir Valley"), but government control in the area did not improve.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGrauGress200226-0)[\[138\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGrauGress200226) Heavy fighting also occurred in the provinces neighbouring Pakistan, where cities and government outposts were constantly besieged by the Mujahideen. Massive Soviet operations would regularly break these sieges, but the Mujahideen would return as soon as the Soviets left.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bear-0)[\[139\]](#refnote-bear) In the west and south, fighting was more sporadic, except in the cities of [Herat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat "Herat") and [Kandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kandahar "Kandahar"), which were always partly controlled by the resistance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Roy1990-2)[\[67\]](#refnote-Roy1990):191

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/c/cd/August_1984_-_captured_field_guns_in_Jaji%2C_Paktia.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:August_1984_-_captured_field_guns_in_Jaji,_Paktia.jpg) Mujahideen with two captured field guns in [Jaji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaji_District "Jaji District"), 1984

The Soviets did not initially foresee taking on such an active role in fighting the rebels and attempted to play down their role there as giving light assistance to the Afghan army. The arrival of the Soviets had the opposite effect as it incensed instead of pacified the people, causing the Mujahideen to gain in strength and numbers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-142-0)[\[140\]](#refnote-142) Originally the Soviets thought that their forces would strengthen the backbone of the Afghan army and provide assistance by securing major cities, lines of communication and transport.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEAmstutz199443-0)[\[141\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEAmstutz199443) The Afghan army forces had a high desertion rate and were loath to fight, especially since the Soviet forces pushed them into infantry roles while they manned the armored vehicles and artillery. The main reason that the Afghan soldiers were so ineffective, though, was poor morale, as many of them were not truly loyal to the communist government but simply wanting a paycheck.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\] Once it became apparent that the Soviets would have to get their hands dirty, they followed three main strategies aimed at quelling the uprising.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEAmstutz1994144-0)[\[142\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEAmstutz1994144) Intimidation was the first strategy, in which the Soviets would use airborne attacks and armored ground attacks to destroy villages, livestock and crops in troubled areas. The Soviets would bomb villages that were near sites of guerrilla attacks on Soviet convoys or known to support resistance groups. Local peoples were forced to either flee their homes or die as daily Soviet attacks made it impossible to live in these areas. By forcing Afghans to flee their homes, the Soviets hoped to deprive the guerrillas of resources and havens. The second strategy consisted of subversion, which entailed sending spies to join resistance groups, as well as bribing local tribes or guerrilla leaders into ceasing operations. Finally, the Soviets used military forays into contested territories to root out the guerrillas and limit their options. Classic search and destroy operations were implemented using [Mil Mi-24](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mil_Mi-24 "Mil Mi-24") helicopter gunships that would provide cover for ground forces in armored vehicles. Once the villages were occupied by Soviet forces, inhabitants who remained were frequently interrogated and tortured for information or killed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Report_from_Afghanistan-0)[\[143\]](#refnote-Report_from_Afghanistan)

> Afghanistan is our [Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_War "Vietnam War"). Look at what has happened. We began by simply backing a friendly regime; slowly we got more deeply involved; then we started manipulating the regime – sometimes using desperate measures – and now? Now we are bogged down in a war we cannot win and cannot abandon.\[...\] but for [Brezhnev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonid_Brezhnev "Leonid Brezhnev") and company we would never have got into it in the first place.

— [Vladimir Kuzichkin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Kuzichkin "Vladimir Kuzichkin"), a [KGB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KGB "KGB") defector, 1982[&#8202;](#ref-back-146-0)[\[144\]](#refnote-146)

To complement their brute force approach to weeding out the insurgency, the Soviets used [KHAD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KHAD "KHAD") (Afghan secret police) to gather intelligence, infiltrate the Mujahideen, spread false information, bribe tribal militias into fighting and organize a government militia. While it is impossible to know exactly how successful KHAD was in infiltrating Mujahideen groups, it is thought that they succeeded in penetrating a good many resistance groups based in Afghanistan, Pakistan and Iran.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-0)[\[145\]](#refnote-:1):149 KHAD is thought to have had particular success in igniting internal rivalries and political divisions amongst the resistance groups, rendering some of them completely useless because of infighting.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Girardet,_Edward_1985_129-0)[\[146\]](#refnote-Girardet,_Edward_1985_129):129 KHAD had some success in securing tribal loyalties but many of these relationships were fickle and temporary. Often KHAD secured neutrality agreements rather than committed political alignment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Girardet,_Edward_1985_129-1)[\[146\]](#refnote-Girardet,_Edward_1985_129):133

The [Sarandoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarandoy "Sarandoy") were a centrally commanded government paramilitary group placed under the control of the [Ministry of Interior Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Interior_Affairs_(Afghanistan) "Ministry of Interior Affairs (Afghanistan)"), before being placed under the control of the unified Ministry of State Security ( [WAD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KHAD "KHAD")) in 1986.[&#8202;](#ref-back-149-0)[\[147\]](#refnote-149) They had mixed success in the war, as the [Arab mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujahideen "Mujahideen") fought the Sarandoy's 7th Operative Regiment, only to fail and sustain massive casualties. The label "Sarandoy" additionally included traffic police, provincial officers and corrections/labor facility officers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Oliker2011-0)[\[148\]](#refnote-Oliker2011)[&#8202;](#ref-back-151-0)[\[149\]](#refnote-151) Large salaries and proper weapons attracted a good number of recruits to the cause, even if they were not necessarily "pro-communist". The problem was that many of the recruits they attracted were in fact Mujahideen who would join up to procure arms, ammunition and money while also gathering information about forthcoming military operations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Girardet,_Edward_1985_129-2)[\[146\]](#refnote-Girardet,_Edward_1985_129):129 By the end of 1981, there were reports of [Bulgarian Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_Armed_Forces "Bulgarian Armed Forces") present in [Mazar-i-Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazar-i-Sharif "Mazar-i-Sharif"), the [Warsaw Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact "Warsaw Pact") and the [Cuban Revolutionary Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Revolutionary_Armed_Forces "Cuban Revolutionary Armed Forces") all operating in [Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan "Afghanistan"). A fighter of the [Mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujahideen "Mujahideen"), describing the [Cubans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cubans "Cubans") in combat, said they were "big and black and shout very loudly when they fight. Unlike the [Russians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russians "Russians") they were not afraid to attack us in the open".[&#8202;](#ref-back-Nyrop1986-1)[\[13\]](#refnote-Nyrop1986)

In 1985, the size of the LCOSF (Limited Contingent of Soviet Forces) was increased to 108,800 and fighting increased throughout the country, making 1985 the bloodiest year of the war. However, despite suffering heavily, the Mujahideen were able to remain in the field, mostly because they received thousands of new volunteers daily, and continued resisting the Soviets

### Reforms of the Karmal administration

[Babrak Karmal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babrak_Karmal "Babrak Karmal"), after the invasion, promised reforms to win support from the population alienated by his ousted predecessors. A temporary constitution, the Fundamental Principles of the Democratic Republic of Afghanistan, was adopted in April 1980. On paper, it was a [democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy "Democracy") constitution including "right of free expression" and disallowing "torture, persecution, and punishment, contrary to human dignity". Karmal's government was formed of his fellow Parchamites along with (pro-Taraki) Khalqists, and a number of known non-communists/leftists in various ministries.[&#8202;](#ref-back-cdlib-2)[\[131\]](#refnote-cdlib)

Karmal called his regime "a new evolutionary phase of the glorious April Revolution", but he failed at uniting the PDPA. In the eyes of many Afghans, he was still seen as a " [puppet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puppet "Puppet")" of the Soviet Union.[&#8202;](#ref-back-cdlib-3)[\[131\]](#refnote-cdlib)[&#8202;](#ref-back-152-0)[\[150\]](#refnote-152)[&#8202;](#ref-back-153-0)[\[151\]](#refnote-153)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEAmstutz1994127-1)[\[135\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEAmstutz1994127)

### Mujahideen insurrection

Main article: [Afghan mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_mujahideen "Afghan mujahideen")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cf/Evstafiev-spetsnaz-prepare-for-mission.jpg/500px-Evstafiev-spetsnaz-prepare-for-mission.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Evstafiev-spetsnaz-prepare-for-mission.jpg) A Soviet [Spetsnaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spetsnaz "Spetsnaz") (special operations) group prepares for a mission in Afghanistan, 1988.

In the mid-1980s, the Afghan resistance movement, assisted by the United States, Pakistan, Saudi Arabia, the United Kingdom, Egypt, the People's Republic of China and others, contributed to Moscow's high military costs and strained international relations. The U.S. viewed the conflict in Afghanistan as an integral Cold War struggle, and the CIA provided assistance to anti-Soviet forces through the [Pakistani intelligence services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_intelligence_services "Pakistani intelligence services"), in a program called [Operation Cyclone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Cyclone "Operation Cyclone").[&#8202;](#ref-back-1986-1992-CIA-AND-BRITISH-RECRUIT-AND-TRAIN-MILITANTS-WORLDWIDE-TO-HELP-FIGHT-AFGHAN-WAR-0)[\[152\]](#refnote-1986-1992-CIA-AND-BRITISH-RECRUIT-AND-TRAIN-MILITANTS-WORLDWIDE-TO-HELP-FIGHT-AFGHAN-WAR)

Pakistan's [North-West Frontier Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khyber_Pakhtunkhwa "Khyber Pakhtunkhwa") became a base for the Afghan resistance fighters and the [Deobandi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deobandi "Deobandi") ulama of that province played a significant role in the Afghan 'jihad', with [Darul Uloom Haqqania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darul_Uloom_Haqqania "Darul Uloom Haqqania") becoming a prominent organisational and networking base for the anti-Soviet Afghan fighters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-155-0)[\[153\]](#refnote-155) As well as money, Muslim countries provided thousands of volunteer fighters known as " [Afghan Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Arabs "Afghan Arabs")", who wished to wage [jihad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jihad "Jihad") against the [atheist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheism "Atheism") communists. Notable among them was a young Saudi named [Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden "Osama bin Laden"), whose [Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab "Arab") group eventually evolved into [al-Qaeda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda "Al-Qaeda").[&#8202;](#ref-back-books.google.com-0)[\[154\]](#refnote-books.google.com):5–8[&#8202;](#ref-back-DID-THE-US-CREATE-OSAMA-BIN-LADEN-0)[\[155\]](#refnote-DID-THE-US-CREATE-OSAMA-BIN-LADEN)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Marshall-0)[\[156\]](#refnote-Marshall) Despite their numbers,[&#8202;](#ref-back-NPR-0)[\[157\]](#refnote-NPR)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Commins-0)[\[158\]](#refnote-Commins)[&#8202;](#ref-back-rashid-0)[\[159\]](#refnote-rashid) the contribution has been called a "curious sideshow to the real fighting,"[&#8202;](#ref-back-162-0)[\[160\]](#refnote-162) with only an estimated 2000 of them fighting "at any one time", compared with about 250,000 Afghan fighters and 125,000 Soviet troops.[&#8202;](#ref-back-163-0)[\[161\]](#refnote-163)

Their efforts were also sometimes counterproductive, as in the March 1989 [battle for Jalalabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Jalalabad_(1989) "Battle of Jalalabad (1989)"), when they showed the enemy the fate awaiting infidels in the form of a truck filled with dismembered bodies of their comrades chopped to pieces after surrendering to radical non-Afghan salafists.[&#8202;](#ref-back-164-0)[\[162\]](#refnote-164) Though demoralized by the abandonment of them by the Soviets, the Afghan Communist government forces rallied to break the siege of Jalalabad and to win the first major government victory in years. "This success reversed the government's demoralization from the withdrawal of Soviet forces, renewed its determination to fight on, and allowed it to survive three more years."[&#8202;](#ref-back-books.google.com-1)[\[154\]](#refnote-books.google.com):58–59

[Maoist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maoist "Maoist") guerrilla groups were also active, to a lesser extent compared to the religious Mujahideen. A notable Maoist group was the [Liberation Organization of the People of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberation_Organization_of_the_People_of_Afghanistan "Liberation Organization of the People of Afghanistan") (SAMA), whose founder and leader Abdul Majid Kalakani was reportedly arrested in 1980.[&#8202;](#ref-back-165-0)[\[163\]](#refnote-165)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c4/Afghanistan_insurgency_1985.png/500px-Afghanistan_insurgency_1985.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Afghanistan_insurgency_1985.png) The areas where the different Mujahideen forces operated in 1985

Afghanistan's resistance movement was born in chaos, spread and triumphed chaotically, and did not find a way to govern differently. Virtually all of its war was waged locally by regional warlords. As warfare became more sophisticated, outside support and regional coordination grew. Even so, the basic units of Mujahideen organization and action continued to reflect the highly segmented nature of Afghan society.[&#8202;](#ref-back-loc1-0)[\[164\]](#refnote-loc1)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/%D0%92%D0%B0%D0%B4%D0%B8%D0%BC_%D0%A7%D1%83%D0%BF%D1%80%D0%B8%D0%BD%D0%B0-%D0%9A%D0%B0%D0%B1%D1%83%D0%BB_VADIM_CHUPRINA_%C2%A9_Kabul_09.jpg/500px-%D0%92%D0%B0%D0%B4%D0%B8%D0%BC_%D0%A7%D1%83%D0%BF%D1%80%D0%B8%D0%BD%D0%B0-%D0%9A%D0%B0%D0%B1%D1%83%D0%BB_VADIM_CHUPRINA_%C2%A9_Kabul_09.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:%D0%92%D0%B0%D0%B4%D0%B8%D0%BC_%D0%A7%D1%83%D0%BF%D1%80%D0%B8%D0%BD%D0%B0-%D0%9A%D0%B0%D0%B1%D1%83%D0%BB_VADIM_CHUPRINA_%C2%A9_Kabul_09.jpg) [Darul Aman Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darul_Aman_Palace "Darul Aman Palace") in 1982, general headquarters of the Afghan Army

[Olivier Roy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olivier_Roy_(professor) "Olivier Roy (professor)") estimates that after four years of war, there were at least 4,000 bases from which Mujahideen units operated. Most of these were affiliated with the seven expatriate parties headquartered in Pakistan, which served as sources of supply and varying degrees of supervision. Significant commanders typically led 300 or more men, controlled several bases and dominated a district or a sub-division of a province. Hierarchies of organization above the bases were attempted. Their operations varied greatly in scope, the most ambitious being achieved by [Ahmad Shah Massoud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Shah_Massoud "Ahmad Shah Massoud") of the [Panjshir valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panjshir_valley "Panjshir valley") north of [Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul "Kabul"). He led at least 10,000 trained troopers at the end of the Soviet war and had expanded his political control of [Tajik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tajiks "Tajiks")-dominated areas to Afghanistan's northeastern provinces under the Supervisory Council of the North.[&#8202;](#ref-back-loc1-1)[\[164\]](#refnote-loc1)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/August_1985_Muja.jpg/500px-August_1985_Muja.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:August_1985_Muja.jpg) Three mujahideen in [Asmar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asmar,_Afghanistan "Asmar, Afghanistan"), 1985

Roy also describes regional, ethnic and sectarian variations in Mujahideen organization. In the [Pashtun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtun_people "Pashtun people") areas of the east, south and southwest, tribal structure, with its many rival sub-divisions, provided the basis for military organization and leadership. Mobilization could be readily linked to traditional fighting allegiances of the tribal _lashkar_ (fighting force). In favorable circumstances such formations could quickly reach more than 10,000, as happened when large Soviet assaults were launched in the eastern provinces, or when the Mujahideen besieged towns, such as [Khost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khost "Khost") in [Paktia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paktia "Paktia") province in July 1983.[&#8202;](#ref-back-167-0)[\[165\]](#refnote-167) But in campaigns of the latter type the traditional explosions of manpower—customarily common immediately after the completion of harvest—proved obsolete when confronted by well dug-in defenders with modern weapons. Lashkar durability was notoriously short; few sieges succeeded.[&#8202;](#ref-back-loc1-2)[\[164\]](#refnote-loc1)

Mujahideen mobilization in non-Pashtun regions faced very different obstacles. Prior to the intervention, few non-Pashtuns possessed firearms. Early in the war they were most readily available from army troops or [gendarmerie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gendarmerie "Gendarmerie") who defected or were ambushed. The international arms market and foreign military support tended to reach the minority areas last. In the northern regions, little military tradition had survived upon which to build an armed resistance. Mobilization mostly came from political leadership closely tied to Islam. Roy contrasts the social leadership of religious figures in the [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_language "Persian language")\- and [Turkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_languages "Turkic languages")-speaking regions of Afghanistan with that of the Pashtuns. Lacking a strong political representation in a state dominated by Pashtuns, minority communities commonly looked to pious learned or charismatically revered _[pirs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pir_(Sufism) "Pir (Sufism)")_ (saints) for leadership. Extensive [Sufi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufi "Sufi") and [maraboutic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maraboutic "Maraboutic") networks were spread through the minority communities, readily available as foundations for leadership, organization, communication and indoctrination. These networks also provided for political mobilization, which led to some of the most effective of the resistance operations during the war.[&#8202;](#ref-back-loc1-3)[\[164\]](#refnote-loc1)

The Mujahideen favoured [sabotage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabotage "Sabotage") operations. The more common types of sabotage included damaging [power lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_power_transmission "Electric power transmission"), knocking out [pipelines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pipeline_transport "Pipeline transport") and radio stations, blowing up government [office buildings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_building "Office building"), [air terminals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_terminal "Air terminal"), hotels, cinemas, and so on. In the border region with Pakistan, the Mujahideen would often launch 800 [rockets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocket "Rocket") per day. Between April 1985 and January 1987, they carried out over 23,500 [shelling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shell_(projectile) "Shell (projectile)") attacks on government targets. The Mujahideen surveyed firing positions that they normally located near villages within the range of Soviet artillery posts, putting the villagers in danger of death from Soviet retaliation. The Mujahideen used [land mines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_mines "Land mines") heavily. Often, they would enlist the services of the local inhabitants, even children.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Mujahideen_prayer_in_Shultan_Valley_Kunar%2C_1987.jpg/500px-Mujahideen_prayer_in_Shultan_Valley_Kunar%2C_1987.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mujahideen_prayer_in_Shultan_Valley_Kunar,_1987.jpg) Mujahideen praying in Shultan Valley, 1987

They concentrated on both civilian and military targets, knocking out bridges, closing major roads, attacking [convoys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convoy "Convoy"), disrupting the electric power system and industrial production, and attacking police stations and Soviet military installations and air bases. They assassinated government officials and PDPA members, and laid siege to small rural outposts. In March 1982, a bomb exploded at the [Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_(government_department) "Ministry (government department)") of Education, damaging several buildings. In the same month, a widespread [power failure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_failure "Power failure") darkened Kabul when a pylon on the transmission line from the Naghlu power station was blown up. In June 1982 a column of about 1,000 young [communist party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_party "Communist party") members sent out to work in the Panjshir valley were ambushed within 30km of Kabul, with heavy loss of life. On 4 September 1985, insurgents shot down a domestic Bakhtar Airlines plane as it took off from Kandahar airport, killing all 52 people aboard.

Mujahideen groups used for assassination had three to five men in each. After they received their mission to kill certain government officials, they busied themselves with studying his pattern of life and its details and then selecting the method of fulfilling their established mission. They practiced shooting at automobiles, [shooting out of automobiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drive-by "Drive-by"), laying mines in government accommodation or houses, using poison, and rigging explosive charges in transport.

In May 1985, the seven principal rebel organizations formed the [Seven Party Mujahideen Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Party_Mujahideen_Alliance "Seven Party Mujahideen Alliance") to coordinate their military operations against the Soviet Army. Late in 1985, the groups were active in and around Kabul, unleashing rocket attacks and conducting operations against the communist government.

#### Raids inside Soviet territory

See also: [United Kingdom in the Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_in_the_Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War "United Kingdom in the Soviet–Afghan War")

[Afghan mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_mujahideen "Afghan mujahideen") raids inside the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union "Soviet Union") during the Soviet–Afghan War were an effort to foment unrest and rebellion by the Muslim populations of the Soviet Union, starting in late 1984 [Director of CIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Director_of_the_Central_Intelligence_Agency "Director of the Central Intelligence Agency") [William Casey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Casey "William Casey") encouraged mujahideen militants to mount sabotage raids inside the Soviet Union, according to [Robert Gates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Gates "Robert Gates"), Casey's executive assistant and Mohammed Yousef, the Pakistani [ISI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-Services_Intelligence "Inter-Services Intelligence") brigadier general who was the chief for Afghan operations. With the help of Britain's [MI6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MI6 "MI6"), the rebels began cross-border raids into the Soviet Union in spring 1985.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEColl2004104-0)[\[166\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEColl2004104)[&#8202;](#ref-back-LSA-0)[\[167\]](#refnote-LSA)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEKaplan2008128-0)[\[168\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEKaplan2008128)

From 1984 in conjunction with the CIA and ISI, MI6 helped organize and execute "scores" of guerrilla-style attacks. These included rocket attacks on villages in [Tajikistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tajik_Soviet_Socialist_Republic "Tajik Soviet Socialist Republic") and raids on Soviet airfields, troop supplies and convoys in [Uzbekistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uzbek_Soviet_Socialist_Republic "Uzbek Soviet Socialist Republic") which flowed through these areas, some 25 kilometers in these territories.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEColes201848-0)[\[169\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEColes201848)

In August 1985, Afghan mujahideen bombed a Soviet military airbase in [Krasnovodsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krasnovodsk "Krasnovodsk"), [Turkmenistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkmen_Soviet_Socialist_Republic "Turkmen Soviet Socialist Republic"). Three soldiers were killed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LSA-1)[\[167\]](#refnote-LSA)\[ _[failed verification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability "Wikipedia:Verifiability")_\] In January 1987 a bomb exploded on a Moscow-bound train in northwestern Uzbekistan, killing three citizens. The attack was likely meant to target Soviet troops.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LSA-2)[\[167\]](#refnote-LSA)\[ _[failed verification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability "Wikipedia:Verifiability")_\] In April 1987 three separate teams of Afghan rebels were directed by the [ISI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-Services_Intelligence "Inter-Services Intelligence") to launch coordinated raids on multiple targets across the Soviet border and extending, in the case of an attack on an Uzbek factory, as deep as over 16 kilometres (10mi) into Soviet territory.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEColl2004161–162-0)[\[170\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEColl2004161–162)

Pakistan's ISI requested [limpet mines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limpet_mines "Limpet mines") from Britain in the hope of attacking Soviet transport barges on the South bank of the [Amu Darya River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amu_Darya_River "Amu Darya River"). MI6 facilitated the attacks which included the limpets. In this they were successful in destroying a number of barges as well as damaging the bridge pylons spanning the river near [Termez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Termez "Termez").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERiedel201448-0)[\[171\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERiedel201448)

CIA director [William Casey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Casey "William Casey") secretly visited Pakistan numerous times to meet with the ISI officers managing the mujahideen,[&#8202;](#ref-back-174-0)[\[172\]](#refnote-174) and personally observed the guerrillas training on at least one occasion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-washingtonpost.com-0)[\[173\]](#refnote-washingtonpost.com) Coll reports that

> Casey startled his Pakistani hosts by proposing that they take the Afghan war into enemy territory—into the Soviet Union itself. Casey wanted to ship subversive propaganda through Afghanistan to the Soviet Union's predominantly Muslim southern republics. The Pakistanis agreed, and the CIA soon supplied thousands of Korans, as well as books on Soviet atrocities in Uzbekistan and tracts on historical heroes of Uzbek nationalism, according to Pakistani and Western officials.[&#8202;](#ref-back-washingtonpost.com-1)[\[173\]](#refnote-washingtonpost.com)

The UK's role in the war entailed direct military involvement not only in Afghanistan but the Central Asian republics of the Soviet Union.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTECormac2018235–36-0)[\[174\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTECormac2018235–36) From 1984 in conjunction with the CIA and ISI, MI6 helped organize and execute "scores" of guerrilla-style attacks. These included rocket attacks on villages in [Tajikistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tajikistan "Tajikistan") and raids on Soviet airfields, troop supplies and convoys in [Uzbekistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uzbekistan "Uzbekistan") which flowed through these areas, some 25 kilometers in these territories.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEColes201848-1)[\[169\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEColes201848) These were the first direct Western attacks on the Soviet Union since the 1950s and they reached their peak in 1986.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTECurtis2010146–47-0)[\[175\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTECurtis2010146–47) MI6 directly remitted money into an account of Pakistani leader of [Jamaat-e-Islami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamaat-e-Islami "Jamaat-e-Islami") [Qazi Hussain Ahmad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qazi_Hussain_Ahmad "Qazi Hussain Ahmad") who had close links with [Hekmatyar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulbuddin_Hekmatyar "Gulbuddin Hekmatyar") and [Massoud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Shah_Massoud "Ahmad Shah Massoud"). MI6's aim was for Ahmad to spread radical and anti-Soviet Islamic literature in the Soviet republics in the hope of rebellions against their Communist governments. These went as far as [Chechnya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Chechnya_and_Ingushetia_(1934%E2%80%931993) "History of Chechnya and Ingushetia (1934–1993)") and [Bosnia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Republic_of_Bosnia_and_Herzegovina "Socialist Republic of Bosnia and Herzegovina").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESareen200574-0)[\[176\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESareen200574) The uprisings did not occur but the Soviets were concerned about potential uprisings during the war and even threatened retaliation with bombings in Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEColes201848-2)[\[169\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEColes201848)

#### Media reaction

> Those hopelessly brave warriors I walked with, and their families, who suffered so much for faith and freedom and who are still not free, they were truly the people of God.

—Journalist [Rob Schultheis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rob_Schultheis "Rob Schultheis"), 1992[&#8202;](#ref-back-179-0)[\[177\]](#refnote-179)[&#8202;](#ref-back-pete-0)[\[178\]](#refnote-pete)

International journalistic perception of the war varied. Major American television journalists were sympathetic to the Mujahideen. Most visible was CBS News correspondent Dan Rather, who in 1982 accused the Soviet Union of genocide, comparing them to Hitler.[&#8202;](#ref-back-181-0)[\[179\]](#refnote-181) Rather was [embedded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embedded_journalism "Embedded journalism") with the Mujahideen for a _[60 Minutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/60_Minutes "60 Minutes")_ report.[&#8202;](#ref-back-182-0)[\[180\]](#refnote-182) In 1987, CBS produced a full documentary special on the war.[&#8202;](#ref-back-183-0)[\[181\]](#refnote-183)[&#8202;](#ref-back-TAYLOR-0)[\[182\]](#refnote-TAYLOR)[&#8202;](#ref-back-nyt-0)[\[183\]](#refnote-nyt)

_[Reader's Digest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reader's_Digest "Reader's Digest")_ took a highly positive view of the Mujahideen, a reversal of their usual view of Islamic fighters. The publication praised their martyrdom and their role in entrapping the Soviets in a Vietnam War-style disaster.[&#8202;](#ref-back-186-0)[\[184\]](#refnote-186)

Leftist journalist [Alexander Cockburn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Cockburn "Alexander Cockburn") was unsympathetic, criticizing Afghanistan as "an unspeakable country filled with unspeakable people, sheepshaggers and smugglers, who have furnished in their leisure hours some of the worst arts and crafts ever to penetrate the occidental world. I yield to none in my sympathy to those prostrate beneath the Russian jackboot, but if ever a country deserved rape it's Afghanistan."[&#8202;](#ref-back-Robin-0)[\[185\]](#refnote-Robin) [Robert D. Kaplan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_D._Kaplan "Robert D. Kaplan") on the other hand, thought any perception of Mujahideen as "barbaric" was unfair: "Documented accounts of mujahidin savagery were relatively rare and involved enemy troops only. Their cruelty toward civilians was unheard of during the war, while Soviet cruelty toward civilians was common."[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEKaplan2008120-0)[\[186\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEKaplan2008120) Lack of interest in the Mujahideen cause, Kaplan believed, was not the lack of intrinsic interest to be found in a war between a small, poor country and a superpower where a million civilians were killed, but the result of the great difficulty and unprofitability of media coverage. Kaplan noted that "none of the American TV networks had a bureau for a war",[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEKaplan200810-0)[\[187\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEKaplan200810) and television cameramen venturing to follow the Mujahideen "trekked for weeks on little food, only to return ill and half starved".[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEKaplan200814-0)[\[188\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEKaplan200814) In October 1984, the Soviet ambassador to Pakistan, Vitaly Smirnov, told [Agence France Presse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agence_France_Presse "Agence France Presse") "that journalists traveling with the mujahidin 'will be killed. And our units in Afghanistan will help the Afghan forces to do it.'"[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEKaplan200810-1)[\[187\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEKaplan200810) Unlike Vietnam and Lebanon, Afghanistan had "absolutely no clash between the strange and the familiar", no "rock-video quality" of "zonked-out GIs in headbands" or "rifle-wielding Shiite terrorists wearing Michael Jackson T-shirts" that provided interesting "visual materials" for newscasts.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEKaplan200815-0)[\[189\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEKaplan200815)

## Soviet exit and change of Afghan leadership, 1985–1989

### Foreign diplomatic efforts

As early as 1983, Pakistan's [Foreign Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Ministry_of_Pakistan "Foreign Ministry of Pakistan") began working with the Soviet Union to provide them an exit from [Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan "Afghanistan"), initiatives led by [Foreign Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Minister_of_Pakistan "Foreign Minister of Pakistan") [Yaqub Ali Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahabzada_Yaqub_Khan "Sahabzada Yaqub Khan") and [Khurshid Kasuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khurshid_Mahmud_Kasuri "Khurshid Mahmud Kasuri"). Despite an active support for [insurgent groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Unity_of_Afghanistan_Mujahideen "Islamic Unity of Afghanistan Mujahideen"), Pakistanis remained sympathetic to the challenges faced by the Soviets in restoring the peace, eventually exploring the possibility of setting up an interim [system of government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/System_of_government "System of government") under former [monarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Afghanistan "King of Afghanistan") [Zahir Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Zahir_Shah "Mohammad Zahir Shah"), but this was not authorized by President [Zia-ul-Haq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Zia-ul-Haq "Muhammad Zia-ul-Haq") due to his stance on the issue of the [Durand Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durand_Line "Durand Line").:247–248[&#8202;](#ref-back-Yale_University_Press,_Rubin-1)[\[77\]](#refnote-Yale_University_Press,_Rubin):248– In 1984–85, Foreign Minister [Yaqub Ali Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahabzada_Yaqub_Khan "Sahabzada Yaqub Khan") paid state visits to China, [Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabia "Saudi Arabia"), Soviet Union, France, United States and the United Kingdom in order to develop a framework. On 20 July 1987, the withdrawal of Soviet troops from the country was announced.

### April 1985 – January 1987: Exit strategy

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Evstafiev-Soviet-soldier-Afghanistan.jpg/500px-Evstafiev-Soviet-soldier-Afghanistan.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Evstafiev-Soviet-soldier-Afghanistan.jpg) Soviet soldier in Afghanistan, 1988

The first step of the Soviet Union's exit strategy was to transfer the burden of fighting the Mujahideen to the Afghan armed forces, with the aim of preparing them to operate without Soviet help. During this phase, the Soviet contingent was restricted to supporting the DRA forces by providing [artillery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artillery "Artillery"), air support and technical assistance, though some large-scale operations were still carried out by Soviet troops.

Under Soviet guidance, the DRA armed forces were built up to an official strength of 302,000 in 1986. To minimize the risk of a coup d'état, they were divided into different branches, each modeled on its Soviet counterpart. The ministry of defence forces numbered 132,000, the ministry of interior 70,000 and the ministry of state security ( [KHAD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KHAD "KHAD")) 80,000. However, these were theoretical figures: in reality each service was plagued with [desertions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desertion "Desertion"), the army alone suffering over 10% annual losses, or 32,000 per year.

The decision to engage primarily Afghan forces was taken by the Soviets, but was resented by the PDPA, who viewed the departure of their protectors without enthusiasm. In May 1987 a DRA force [attacked well-entrenched Mujahideen positions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Arghandab_(1987) "Battle of Arghandab (1987)") in the [Arghandab District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arghandab_District "Arghandab District"), but the Mujahideen managed to hold their ground, and the attackers suffered heavy casualties.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-1)[\[145\]](#refnote-:1):227 Meanwhile, the Mujahideen benefited from expanded foreign military support from the United States, United Kingdom, Saudi Arabia, Pakistan, and other Muslim-majority countries. Two [Heritage Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Heritage_Foundation "The Heritage Foundation") foreign policy analysts, Michael Johns and James A. Phillips, championed [Ahmad Shah Massoud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Shah_Massoud "Ahmad Shah Massoud") as the Afghan resistance leader most worthy of US support under the [Reagan Doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reagan_Doctrine "Reagan Doctrine").[&#8202;](#ref-back-192-0)[\[190\]](#refnote-192)[&#8202;](#ref-back-193-0)[\[191\]](#refnote-193)[&#8202;](#ref-back-194-0)[\[192\]](#refnote-194)

### May 1986 – 1988: Najibullah and his reforms

The government of President Karmal, a [puppet state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puppet_state "Puppet state"), was largely ineffective. It was weakened by divisions within the PDPA and the Parcham faction, and the regime's efforts to expand its base of support proved futile. Moscow came to regard Karmal as a failure and blamed him for the problems. Years later, when Karmal's inability to consolidate his government had become obvious, Mikhail Gorbachev, then General Secretary of the [Soviet Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Communist_Party "Soviet Communist Party"), said, "The main reason that there has been no national consolidation so far is that Comrade Karmal is hoping to continue sitting in Kabul with our help."[&#8202;](#ref-back-195-0)[\[193\]](#refnote-195) Karmal's consolidation plan only involved those who had not raised arms against the regime, and even demanded Soviet troops to seal the border with Pakistan before any negotiations with Mujahideen. Eventually, the Soviet Union decided to dispose of Karmal from the leadership of Afghanistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-cdlib-4)[\[131\]](#refnote-cdlib)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/RIAN_archive_644461_First_stage_in_the_Soviet_troop_withdrawal_from_Afghanistan.jpg/500px-RIAN_archive_644461_First_stage_in_the_Soviet_troop_withdrawal_from_Afghanistan.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:RIAN_archive_644461_First_stage_in_the_Soviet_troop_withdrawal_from_Afghanistan.jpg) A column of Soviet BTR armored personnel carriers departing from Afghanistan

In May 1986, [Mohammad Najibullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Najibullah "Mohammad Najibullah"), former chief of the Afghan [secret police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secret_police "Secret police") ( [KHAD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KHAD "KHAD")), was elected General Secretary and later as President of the Revolutionary Council. The relatively young new leader wasn't known that well to the Afghan population at the time, but he made swift reforms to change the country's situation and win support as devised by experts of the [Communist Party of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_the_Soviet_Union "Communist Party of the Soviet Union"). An eloquent speaker in both the Pashto and Dari languages, Najibullah engaged with elders and presented both himself and the state as Islamic, sometimes backing his speeches with excerpts from the [Qur'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qur'an "Qur'an"). A number of prisoners were released, while the night curfew in Kabul that had been in place since 1980 was finally lifted. He also moved against pro-Karmal Parchamites, who were expelled from the Revolutionary Council and the Politburo.[&#8202;](#ref-back-cdlib-5)[\[131\]](#refnote-cdlib)

President Najibullah launched the " [National Reconciliation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Reconciliation_(Afghanistan) "National Reconciliation (Afghanistan)")" program at the start of 1987, the goal of which was to unite the nation and end the war that had enveloped the nation for seven years. He expressed willingness to negotiate with the Mujahideen resistance, allow parties other than the PDPA to be active, and indicated that exiled King [Zahir Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zahir_Shah "Zahir Shah") could be part of the process. A six-month ceasefire also began in December 1986. His administration was also more open to foreign visitors outside the Soviet bloc.[&#8202;](#ref-back-cdlib-6)[\[131\]](#refnote-cdlib) In November 1987, Najibullah convened a _[loya jirga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loya_jirga "Loya jirga")_ selected by the authorities which successfully passed a new constitution for Afghanistan, creating a presidential system with an elective bicameral parliament. The constitution declared "the sacred religion of Islam" the official religion, guaranteed the democratic rights of the individual, made it legal to form "political parties", and promoted equality between the various tribes and nationalities.[&#8202;](#ref-back-cdlib-7)[\[131\]](#refnote-cdlib) Despite high expectations, the new policy only had limited impact in regaining support from the population and the resistance, partly because of the high distrust and unpopularity of the PDPA and KHAD, as well as Najibullah's loyalty to Moscow.[&#8202;](#ref-back-cdlib-8)[\[131\]](#refnote-cdlib)

As part of the new structure, national [parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Afghan_parliamentary_election "1988 Afghan parliamentary election") were held in 1988 to elect members of the new National Assembly, the first such elections in Afghanistan in 19 years.

#### Negotiations for a coalition

Ex-king Zahir Shah remained a popular figure to most Afghans. [Diego Cordovez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diego_Cordovez_(United_Nations)?action=edit&redlink=1 "Diego Cordovez (United Nations)") of the UN also recognized the king as a potential key to a political settlement to the war after the Soviet troops would leave. Polls in 1987 showed that he was a favored figure to lead a potential coalition between the DRA regime and Mujahideen factions, as well as an opposition to the unpopular but powerful guerrilla leader [Gulbadin Hekmatyar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulbadin_Hekmatyar "Gulbadin Hekmatyar"), who was strongly against the King's return. Pakistan however was against this and refused to grant the ex-king a visa for potential negotiations with Mujahideen. Pakistan's President [Zia-ul-Haq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Zia-ul-Haq "Muhammad Zia-ul-Haq") and his supporters in the military were determined to put a conservative Islamic ally in power in Kabul.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nsaessay-1)[\[125\]](#refnote-nsaessay)

Negotiations continued and in 1988 through 1989, The Interim Afghan Government was formed in [Pekhawar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peshawar "Peshawar") as an alliance of various Mujahadeen groups including [Hizb-i Islami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hizb-i-Islami_Afghanistan "Hizb-i-Islami Afghanistan") and [Jamiat-i Islami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamiat-i_Islami_Afghanistan "Jamiat-i Islami Afghanistan"), and would be involved in [Operation Arrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Arrow "Operation Arrow") and the [siege of Khost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Khost "Siege of Khost").[&#8202;](#ref-back-196-0)[\[194\]](#refnote-196)[&#8202;](#ref-back-197-0)[\[195\]](#refnote-197)

### April 1988: The Geneva Accords

Main article: [Geneva Accords (1988)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Accords_(1988) "Geneva Accords (1988)")

Following lengthy negotiations, the [Geneva Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Accords_(1988) "Geneva Accords (1988)") was signed in 1988 between Afghanistan and Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEAmstutz1994335-0)[\[196\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEAmstutz1994335) Supported by the Soviet Union and the United States respectively, the two Asian countries agreed to refrain from any form of interference in each other's territory. They also agreed to allow Afghan refugees in Pakistan to voluntarily return. The two superpowers agreed to halt their interference in Afghanistan, which included a Soviet withdrawal.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nsaessay-2)[\[125\]](#refnote-nsaessay)

The United Nations set up a special [mission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Good_Offices_Mission_in_Afghanistan_and_Pakistan "United Nations Good Offices Mission in Afghanistan and Pakistan") to oversee the process. In this way, President Najibullah had stabilized his political position enough to begin matching Moscow's moves toward withdrawal. Among other things the Geneva Accords identified the US and Soviet non-intervention in the internal affairs of Pakistan and Afghanistan and a timetable for full Soviet withdrawal. The agreement on withdrawal held, and on 15 February 1989, the last Soviet troops departed on schedule from Afghanistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-199-0)[\[197\]](#refnote-199)

### January 1987 – February 1989: Withdrawal

Main article: [Soviet withdrawal from Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_withdrawal_from_Afghanistan "Soviet withdrawal from Afghanistan")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/T-62_withdraws_from_Afghanistan.JPEG/500px-T-62_withdraws_from_Afghanistan.JPEG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:T-62_withdraws_from_Afghanistan.JPEG) Soviet [T-62](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T-62 "T-62") M main battle tank withdraws from Afghanistan

The promotion of [Mikhail Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev "Mikhail Gorbachev") to General Secretary in 1985 and his 'new thinking' on foreign and domestic policy was likely an important factor in the Soviets' decision to withdraw. Gorbachev had been attempting to remove the Soviet Union from the economic stagnation that had set in under the leadership of Brezhnev, and to reform the Soviet Union's economy and image with the [Glasnost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glasnost "Glasnost") and [Perestroika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perestroika "Perestroika") policies. Gorbachev had also been attempting to ease cold war tensions by signing the [Intermediate-Range Nuclear Forces Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intermediate-Range_Nuclear_Forces_Treaty "Intermediate-Range Nuclear Forces Treaty") with the U.S. in 1987 and withdrawing the troops from Afghanistan, whose presence had garnered so much international condemnation. Beijing had stipulated that a normalization of relations would have to wait until Moscow withdrew its army from Afghanistan (among other things), and in 1989 the first Sino-Soviet summit in 30 years took place.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMaleySaikal1989127-0)[\[198\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMaleySaikal1989127) At the same time, Gorbachev pressured his Cuban allies in Angola to scale down activities and withdraw even though Soviet allies were faring somewhat better there.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-2)[\[145\]](#refnote-:1):300 The Soviets also pulled many of their troops out of [Mongolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongolia "Mongolia") in 1987, where they were also having a far easier time than in Afghanistan, and restrained the [Vietnamese invasion of Kampuchea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian%E2%80%93Vietnamese_War "Cambodian–Vietnamese War") to the point of an all-out withdrawal in 1988.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMaleySaikal1989132-0)[\[199\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMaleySaikal1989132) This massive withdrawal of Soviet forces from such highly contested areas shows that the Soviet government's decision to leave Afghanistan was based upon a general change in Soviet foreign policy – from one of confrontation to avoidance of conflict wherever possible.[&#8202;](#ref-back-britannica2001-0)[\[200\]](#refnote-britannica2001)[&#8202;](#ref-back-britannica1978-0)[\[201\]](#refnote-britannica1978)

In the last phase, Soviet troops prepared and executed their withdrawal from Afghanistan, whilst limiting the launching of offensive operations by those who had not withdrawn yet.

By mid-1987 the Soviet Union announced that it would start withdrawing its forces. [Sibghatullah Mojaddedi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sibghatullah_Mojaddedi "Sibghatullah Mojaddedi") was selected as the head of the Interim Islamic State of Afghanistan, in an attempt to reassert its legitimacy against the Moscow-sponsored Kabul regime. Mojaddedi, as head of the Interim Afghan Government, met with then-Vice President of the United States [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush "George H. W. Bush"), achieving a critical diplomatic victory for the Afghan resistance. Defeat of the Kabul government was their solution for peace. This confidence, sharpened by their distrust of the United Nations, virtually guaranteed their refusal to accept a political compromise.

In September 1988, Soviet MiG-23 fighters shot down two Iranian [AH-1J Cobra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bell_AH-1_SuperCobra "Bell AH-1 SuperCobra") helicopters which had intruded into Afghan airspace.[&#8202;](#ref-back-204-0)[\[202\]](#refnote-204)

[Operation Magistral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Magistral "Operation Magistral") was one of the final offensive operations undertaken by the Soviets, a successful sweep operation that cleared the road between the towns of [Gardez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gardez "Gardez") and [Khost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khost "Khost"). This operation did not have any lasting effect on the outcome of the conflict nor on the soiled political and military status of the Soviets in the eyes of the West but was a symbolic gesture that marked the end of their widely condemned presence in the country with a victory.[&#8202;](#ref-back-205-0)[\[203\]](#refnote-205)

The first half of the Soviet contingent was withdrawn from 15 May to 16 August 1988. The withdrawal was suspended by the Soviets on 5 November 1988 due to attacks on Soviet troops.[&#8202;](#ref-back-206-0)[\[204\]](#refnote-206) The second half of the Soviet contingent was withdrawn from 15 November to 15 February 1989. In order to ensure a safe passage, the Soviets had negotiated ceasefires with local Mujahideen commanders. The withdrawal was generally executed peacefully[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-3)[\[145\]](#refnote-:1):251 except for the operation "Typhoon".

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/RIAN_archive_660671_Pullout_of_Soviet_troops_contingent_from_Afghanistan.jpg/500px-RIAN_archive_660671_Pullout_of_Soviet_troops_contingent_from_Afghanistan.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:RIAN_archive_660671_Pullout_of_Soviet_troops_contingent_from_Afghanistan.jpg) [CGen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonel_General "Colonel General") of [40th Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/40th_Army_(Soviet_Union) "40th Army (Soviet Union)"), [Boris Gromov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Gromov "Boris Gromov"), announcing the withdrawal of Soviet contingent forces

General Yazov, the [Defense Minister of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Defence_(Soviet_Union) "Minister of Defence (Soviet Union)"), ordered the 40th Army to violate the agreement with [Ahmad Shah Massoud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Shah_Massoud "Ahmad Shah Massoud"), who commanded a large force in the Panjshir Valley, and attack his relaxed and exposed forces. The Soviet attack was initiated to protect Najibullah, who did not have a ceasefire in effect with Massoud, and who rightly feared an offensive by Massoud's forces after the Soviet withdrawal.[&#8202;](#ref-back-fmso.leavenworth.army.mil-0)[\[205\]](#refnote-fmso.leavenworth.army.mil) General Gromov, the 40th Army Commander, objected to the operation, but reluctantly obeyed the order. "Typhoon" began on 23 January and continued for three days. To minimize their own losses, the Soviets abstained from close-range fighting. Instead, they used long-range artillery, surface-to-surface and air-to-surface missiles. Numerous civilian casualties were reported. Massoud had not threatened the withdrawal to this point and did not attack Soviet forces after they breached the agreement.[&#8202;](#ref-back-fmso.leavenworth.army.mil-1)[\[205\]](#refnote-fmso.leavenworth.army.mil) Overall, the Soviet attack represented a defeat for Massoud's forces, who lost 600 fighters killed and wounded.[&#8202;](#ref-back-fmso.leavenworth.army.mil-2)[\[205\]](#refnote-fmso.leavenworth.army.mil)

After the withdrawal of the Soviets, the [DRA forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_Forces_of_the_Democratic_Republic_of_Afghanistan "Armed Forces of the Democratic Republic of Afghanistan") were left fighting alone and had to abandon some provincial capitals, as well as disbanding their [air assault brigades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ANA_Special_Operations_Command "ANA Special Operations Command") a year prior.[&#8202;](#ref-back-208-0)[\[206\]](#refnote-208)[&#8202;](#ref-back-209-0)[\[207\]](#refnote-209) A year prior to the Soviet withdrawal, the Afghan government established the Guards Corps, being renamed to the Special Guard (' _Gard-e Khas_'), Republican Guard, and later, the Afghan National Guard. This force included the 22nd and 44th Guard Regiment, as well as the 88th Heavy Artillery Regiment and the 1st Motorized Infantry Brigade.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-4)[\[145\]](#refnote-:1):227[&#8202;](#ref-back-:2-0)[\[208\]](#refnote-:2) By 1988, the Afghan National Guard had approximately 14,000 officers and personnel organised into two divisions, three infantry brigades, an artillery battalion, a SCUD battalion, and various other units, all equipped with modern weapons and equipment. The force was headquartered at [Bala Hissar Fort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bala_Hissar,_Kabul "Bala Hissar, Kabul").[&#8202;](#ref-back-211-0)[\[209\]](#refnote-211)

It was widely believed that they would not be able to resist the Mujahideen for long. However, in the spring of 1989, DRA forces inflicted a major defeat on the Mujahideen during the [Battle of Jalalabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Jalalabad_(1989) "Battle of Jalalabad (1989)"), as well launching successful assaults on fortified complexes in [Paghman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paghman "Paghman") in 1990.[&#8202;](#ref-back-212-0)[\[210\]](#refnote-212)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:2-1)[\[208\]](#refnote-:2)[&#8202;](#ref-back-213-0)[\[211\]](#refnote-213) The United States, having achieved its goal of forcing the Soviet Union's withdrawal from Afghanistan, gradually disengaged itself from the country.[&#8202;](#ref-back-cdlib-9)[\[131\]](#refnote-cdlib)

#### Causes of withdrawal

Some of the causes of the Soviet Union's withdrawal from Afghanistan leading to the Afghanistan regime's eventual defeat include[&#8202;](#ref-back-Fremont-Barnes-0)[\[212\]](#refnote-Fremont-Barnes)

- The Soviet Army of 1980 was trained and equipped for large scale, conventional warfare in Central Europe against a similar opponent, i.e., it used armored and motor-rifle formations. This was notably ineffective against small scale guerrilla groups using hit-and-run tactics in the rough terrain of Afghanistan. Also, the Soviet Army's large formations were not mobile enough to engage small groups of Mujahideen fighters that easily merged back into the terrain.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Fremont-Barnes-1)[\[212\]](#refnote-Fremont-Barnes) The set strategy also meant that troops were discouraged from "tactical initiative", essential in counter insurgency, because it "tended to upset operational timing".[&#8202;](#ref-back-Grau-0)[\[213\]](#refnote-Grau)
- The Soviets used large-scale offensives against Mujahideen strongholds, such as in the Panjshir Valley, which temporarily cleared those sectors and killed many civilians in addition to enemy combatants. The biggest shortcoming here, though, was the fact that once the Soviets engaged the enemy with force, they failed to hold their ground, as they withdrew once their operation was completed. The killing of civilians further alienated the population from the Soviets, with bad long-term effects.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Fremont-Barnes-2)[\[212\]](#refnote-Fremont-Barnes)
- The Soviets did not have enough men to fight a counter-insurgency war (COIN),[&#8202;](#ref-back-Grau-1)[\[213\]](#refnote-Grau) and their troops had low morale. The peak number of Soviet troops during the war was 115,000, but the bulk of these troops were conscripts, which led to poor combat performance in their motor-rifle formations. However, the Soviets did have elite infantry units, such as the famed Spetsnaz, the VDV, and their recon infantry. The problem with their elite units was not combat effectiveness, but that there were not enough of them and that they were employed incorrectly.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Fremont-Barnes-3)[\[212\]](#refnote-Fremont-Barnes)
- Intelligence gathering, essential for successful COIN, was inadequate. The Soviets overly relied on less-than-accurate aerial recon and radio intercepts rather than their recon infantry and special forces. Although their special forces and recon infantry units performed very well in combat against the Mujahideen, they would have better served in intelligence gathering.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Fremont-Barnes-4)[\[212\]](#refnote-Fremont-Barnes)
- The concept of a "war of national liberation" against a Soviet-sponsored "revolutionary" regime was so alien to the Soviet dogma that the leadership could not "come to grips" with it. This led to, among other things, a suppression by the Soviet media for several years of the truth about how bad the war was going, which caused a backlash this could no longer be hidden.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Grau-2)[\[213\]](#refnote-Grau)

## Fall of Najibullah government, 1992

Main article: [Afghan Civil War (1989–1992)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Civil_War_(1989%E2%80%931992) "Afghan Civil War (1989–1992)")

After the withdrawal of Soviet troops in 1989, the government of [Mohammad Najibullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Najibullah "Mohammad Najibullah") remained in power until 15 April 1992. Najibullah stepped down that day as Mujahideen guerrilla forces moved into Kabul during the first Afghan civil war. He attempted to fly to India under the protection of the U.N. but was blocked from leaving at the airport.[&#8202;](#ref-back-216-0)[\[214\]](#refnote-216)[&#8202;](#ref-back-217-0)[\[215\]](#refnote-217) He then took refuge at a United Nations compound in Kabul. After a bloody, four-year power struggle between different factions of the victorious anti-Najibullah forces, the [Taliban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliban "Taliban") took Kabul. They stormed the U.N. compound on 26 September 1996. They eventually tortured and killed Najibullah.[&#8202;](#ref-back-218-0)[\[216\]](#refnote-218)

## Aerial engagements

See also: [List of Soviet aircraft losses during the Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Soviet_aircraft_losses_during_the_Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War "List of Soviet aircraft losses during the Soviet–Afghan War")

### Aerial losses in Pakistan airspace

Main article: [Spillover of Soviet - Afghan war in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spillover_of_Soviet_-_Afghan_war_in_Pakistan "Spillover of Soviet - Afghan war in Pakistan")

During the conflict, Pakistan Air Force F-16s shot down ten aircraft belonging to Soviet Union, which had intruded into Pakistani territory. However, the Soviet record only confirmed five kills (three Su-22s, one Su-25 and one An-26). Some sources show that PAF had shot down at least a dozen more aircraft during the war. However, those kills were not officially acknowledged because they took place in Afghanistan's airspace and acknowledging those kills would mean that Afghan airspace was violated by PAF.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nationalinterest-1)[\[9\]](#refnote-nationalinterest) In all, Pakistan Air Force F-16s had downed several MiG-23s, Su-22s, a Su-25, and an An-24 while losing only one F-16.

### Stinger missiles and the "Stinger effect"

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/First_Sting.jpg/500px-First_Sting.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:First_Sting.jpg) Painting of the first Stinger Missile kill in 1986

Whether the introduction of the personal, portable, infrared-homing [Stinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIM-92_Stinger "FIM-92 Stinger") [surface-to-air missile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surface-to-air_missile "Surface-to-air missile") in September 1986 was a turning point in the war is disputed. Many Western military analysts credit the Stinger with a [kill ratio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kill_ratio "Kill ratio") of about 70% and with responsibility for most of the over 350 Soviet or Afghan government planes and helicopters shot down in the last two years of the war.[&#8202;](#ref-back-219-0)[\[217\]](#refnote-219) Some military analysts considered it fundamental and coined the term "Stinger effect" to describe it.[&#8202;](#ref-back-220-0)[\[218\]](#refnote-220) [Congressman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Congress "US Congress") [Charlie Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlie_Wilson_(Texas_politician) "Charlie Wilson (Texas politician)") claimed that before the Stinger the Mujahideen never won a [set piece battle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Set_piece_battle "Set piece battle") with the Soviets, but after it was introduced, the Mujahideen never again lost one.[&#8202;](#ref-back-221-0)[\[219\]](#refnote-221)

The statistics are based on Mujahideen self-reporting, which is of unknown reliability. A Russian general claimed the United States "greatly exaggerated" Soviet and Afghan aircraft losses during the war. According to Soviet figures, in 1987–1988, only 35 planes and 63 helicopters were destroyed by all causes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-222-0)[\[220\]](#refnote-222) The [Pakistan Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Army "Pakistan Army") fired twenty-eight Stingers at Soviet aircraft near the border without a kill.[&#8202;](#ref-back-dtic.mil-1)[\[11\]](#refnote-dtic.mil)

Many Russian military analysts tend to be dismissive of the impact of the Stinger. [Soviet General Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Secretary_of_the_Communist_Party_of_the_Soviet_Union "General Secretary of the Communist Party of the Soviet Union") [Mikhail Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev "Mikhail Gorbachev") decided to withdraw from Afghanistan a year before the Mujahideen fired their first Stinger missiles; Gorbachev was motivated by U.S. sanctions, not military losses. The Stingers did make an impact at first but within a few months flares, beacons, and exhaust baffles were installed to disorient the missiles, while night operation and terrain-hugging tactics tended to prevent the rebels from getting a clear shot. By 1988 the Mujahideen had all but stopped firing them.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Kuperman-2002-CFR-0)[\[221\]](#refnote-Kuperman-2002-CFR) Stingers also forced Soviet helicopters and ground attack planes to bomb from higher altitudes with less accuracy but did not bring down many more aircraft than Chinese heavy machine guns and other less sophisticated anti-aircraft weaponry.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Steele-0)[\[222\]](#refnote-Steele) Gorbachev stated in an interview in 2010 that the Stinger did not influence his decision-making process.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Braithwaite2011-1)[\[98\]](#refnote-Braithwaite2011):205,369

## War crimes

This section is an excerpt from [Soviet war crimes in the Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_war_crimes_in_the_Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War "Soviet war crimes in the Soviet–Afghan War").\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Soviet_war_crimes_in_the_Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War&action=edit)\]

[Atrocity crimes in the Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_war_crimes_in_the_Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War "Soviet war crimes in the Soviet–Afghan War") were systematically perpetrated on a large scale by the Soviet Union and its allies from 1979 to 1989, with several scholars and academics concluding that the Soviet military forces carried out a campaign of [genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocide "Genocide") against [Afghans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghans "Afghans").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Soviet_war_crimes_in_the_Soviet–Afghan_War_Kakar_215-0)[\[223\]](#refnote-Soviet_war_crimes_in_the_Soviet–Afghan_War_Kakar_215)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Soviet_war_crimes_in_the_Soviet–Afghan_War_Reisman1-0)[\[224\]](#refnote-Soviet_war_crimes_in_the_Soviet–Afghan_War_Reisman1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-227-0)[\[225\]](#refnote-227) The war resulted in the deaths of between 1,000,000 and 3,000,000 Afghans.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEJoes2010211-1)[\[31\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEJoes2010211) Estimates of Afghan civilian deaths vary from 562,000[&#8202;](#ref-back-Soviet_war_crimes_in_the_Soviet–Afghan_War_562k-0)[\[226\]](#refnote-Soviet_war_crimes_in_the_Soviet–Afghan_War_562k) to 2,000,000.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEKlass2018129-1)[\[29\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEKlass2018129)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGoodson20115-1)[\[30\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGoodson20115) [Human Rights Watch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Watch "Human Rights Watch") concluded that the Soviet Red Army and the Afghan Army perpetrated war crimes and [crimes against humanity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimes_against_humanity "Crimes against humanity") in Afghanistan, intentionally targeting civilians and civilian areas for attack, and killing and torturing prisoners.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Soviet_war_crimes_in_the_Soviet–Afghan_War_hrw2005-0)[\[227\]](#refnote-Soviet_war_crimes_in_the_Soviet–Afghan_War_hrw2005)

## Foreign involvement

Main article: [Foreign involvement in the Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_involvement_in_the_Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War "Foreign involvement in the Soviet–Afghan War")

The [Afghan mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_mujahideen "Afghan mujahideen") were backed primarily by [Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan "Pakistan"), the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States "United States"), [Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabia "Saudi Arabia"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-234-0)[\[232\]](#refnote-234) and the [United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom "United Kingdom") making it a [Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War "Cold War") [proxy war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proxy_war "Proxy war").[&#8202;](#ref-back-235-0)[\[233\]](#refnote-235) Out of the countries that supported the Mujahideen, the U.S. (with [Operation Cyclone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Cyclone "Operation Cyclone")) and Saudi Arabia offered the greatest financial support.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Wilson-0)[\[230\]](#refnote-Wilson)[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto-0)[\[234\]](#refnote-auto)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline-0)[\[235\]](#refnote-Timeline) However, private donors and religious charities throughout the [Muslim world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world "Muslim world")—particularly in the Persian Gulf—raised considerably more funds for the Afghan rebels than any foreign government;[&#8202;](#ref-back-shichor-0)[\[236\]](#refnote-shichor)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEKepel2002143-0)[\[237\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEKepel2002143)[&#8202;](#ref-back-240-0)[\[238\]](#refnote-240) [Jason Burke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jason_Burke "Jason Burke") recounts that "as little as 25 per cent of the money for the Afghan jihad was actually supplied directly by states."[&#8202;](#ref-back-241-0)[\[239\]](#refnote-241) Saudi Arabia was heavily involved in the war effort and matched the United States' contributions dollar-for-dollar in public funds. Saudi Arabia also gathered an enormous amount of money for the Afghan mujahideen in private donations that amounted to about $20 million per month at their peak.[&#8202;](#ref-back-242-0)[\[240\]](#refnote-242)[&#8202;](#ref-back-562k-1)[\[27\]](#refnote-562k) The United Kingdom on the other hand [played more of a direct combat role](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_in_the_Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War "United Kingdom in the Soviet–Afghan War"), through [MI6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MI6 "MI6") and the [Special Air Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Air_Service "Special Air Service") — supporting resistance groups in practical manners.[&#8202;](#ref-back-243-0)[\[241\]](#refnote-243)[&#8202;](#ref-back-244-0)[\[242\]](#refnote-244)

Other countries that supported the Mujahideen were [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt "Egypt") and [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China "China").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTECrile2003131–132-0)[\[243\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTECrile2003131–132) [Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran "Iran") on the other hand only supported the Shia Mujahideen, namely the Persian-speaking Shia [Hazara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazaras "Hazaras") groups in a limited way. One of these groups was the [Tehran Eight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran_Eight "Tehran Eight"), a political union of Afghan Shia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ruttig-0)[\[244\]](#refnote-ruttig)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGoodson2011139-0)[\[245\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGoodson2011139) They were supplied predominantly by the [Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Revolutionary_Guard_Corps "Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps") (IRGC), but Iran's support for the Hazaras nevertheless frustrated efforts for a united Mujahideen front.[&#8202;](#ref-back-248-0)[\[246\]](#refnote-248)

## Spillover

### Raids inside the Soviet Union

Main article: [Raids inside the Soviet Union during the Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raids_inside_the_Soviet_Union_during_the_Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War "Raids inside the Soviet Union during the Soviet–Afghan War")

The Mujahideen launched raids into the Soviet Union to foment unrest and rebellion by the Islamic populations of the Soviet Union, starting in late 1984 [Director of CIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Director_of_the_Central_Intelligence_Agency "Director of the Central Intelligence Agency") [William Casey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Casey "William Casey") as well as Britain's [MI6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MI6 "MI6"), encouraged Mujahideen militants to mount sabotage raids inside the Soviet Union, according to [Robert Gates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Gates "Robert Gates"), Casey's executive assistant and Mohammed Yousef, the Pakistani [Inter-Services Intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-Services_Intelligence "Inter-Services Intelligence") (ISI) brigadier general who was the chief for Afghan operations. The rebels began cross-border raids into the Soviet Union in the spring of 1985.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEColl2004104-1)[\[166\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEColl2004104)[&#8202;](#ref-back-LSA-3)[\[167\]](#refnote-LSA)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEKaplan2008128-1)[\[168\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEKaplan2008128)

### Engagement in Pakistan

Main article: [Spillover of the Soviet–Afghan War in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spillover_of_the_Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War_in_Pakistan "Spillover of the Soviet–Afghan War in Pakistan")

During the conflict, Soviet aircraft intruded into Pakistani airspace multiple times and Pakistan Air Force F-16 had shot down ten aircraft belonging to Soviet Union, which had intruded into Pakistani territory. However, the Soviet record only confirmed five plane kills (three Su-22s, one Su-25 and one An-26) and 4 helicopter (Mi-8) kills. Some sources show that PAF had shot down at least a dozen more aircraft during the war. However, those kills were not officially acknowledged because they took place in Afghanistan's airspace and acknowledging those kills would mean that Afghan airspace was violated by PAF.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nationalinterest-2)[\[9\]](#refnote-nationalinterest) In all, Pakistan Air Force F-16s had downed 3 [Su-22](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Su-22 "Su-22"),1 [Su-25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Su-25 "Su-25"),2 [Mig-23](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mig-23 "Mig-23"),2 [An-26](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/An-26 "An-26"), and Several [Mi-8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mi-8 "Mi-8") while 1 [Mig-23](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mig-23 "Mig-23") was damaged while losing only one [F-16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-16 "F-16") to friendly fire.[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto-1)[\[234\]](#refnote-auto)

#### Terror campaign in Pakistan

The KhAD-KGB campaign in Pakistan was a joint campaign in which the [Afghan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Republic_of_Afghanistan "Democratic Republic of Afghanistan") [KhAD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KHAD "KHAD")'s foreign Tenth Directorate[&#8202;](#ref-back-Oliker2011-1)[\[148\]](#refnote-Oliker2011) and the Soviet [KGB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KGB "KGB") targeted Pakistan using prostitution spy rings, terror attacks, hijackings, serial killings, assassinations and the dissemination of propaganda to dissuade Pakistan from supporting the Afghan Mujahideen.[&#8202;](#ref-back-249-0)[\[247\]](#refnote-249)

#### Miram Shah incident

On 2 April 1986, the 38th commando brigade of [Democratic Republic of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Republic_of_Afghanistan "Democratic Republic of Afghanistan") accidentally landed inside Pakistan territory during the [Second Battle of Zhawar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Battle_of_Zhawar "Second Battle of Zhawar"). The strike force, in the darkness of night, accidentally landed near [Miram Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miram_Shah "Miram Shah") in Pakistan instead of [Zhawar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhawar "Zhawar"). The force was surrounded and 120 soldiers were taken prisoner and six [Mi-8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mi-8 "Mi-8") helicopters were captured.

#### Badaber uprising

Main article: [Badaber uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badaber_uprising "Badaber uprising")

In between 26 and 27 April 1985, in [Badaber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badaber "Badaber"), Pakistan, an armed rebellion was instigated by Soviet and Afghan [prisoners of war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prisoners_of_war "Prisoners of war") who were being held at the Badaber fortress near [Peshawar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peshawar "Peshawar"), Pakistan. The prisoners fought the [Afghan Mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Mujahideen "Afghan Mujahideen") of the [Jamiat-e Islami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamiat-e_Islami "Jamiat-e Islami") party and the Pakistani [XI Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/XI_Corps_(Pakistan) "XI Corps (Pakistan)") supported by American CIA advisors in an attempt to escape but the rebellion was quashed and all POWs were killed.

### Raid inside Iran

Main article: [1982 Harmak incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_Harmak_incident "1982 Harmak incident")

On 5 April 1982, Soviet forces accidentally infiltrated Iranian territory, in which Soviet forces strayed from the target of a Mujahideen base in southern Afghanistan and accidentally destroyed an asphalt factory in [Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran "Iran").[&#8202;](#ref-back-250-0)[\[248\]](#refnote-250) Iranian security forces attacked this strike force using tanks and aircraft, destroying two Soviet [Mi-8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mi-8 "Mi-8") helicopters and damaging many more.

## Impact

### Soviet personnel strengths and casualties
