[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Demonstranten_met_protestborden_achter_een_dranghek%2C_Bestanddeelnr_933-5236.jpg/500px-Demonstranten_met_protestborden_achter_een_dranghek%2C_Bestanddeelnr_933-5236.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Demonstranten_met_protestborden_achter_een_dranghek,_Bestanddeelnr_933-5236.jpg) A demonstration against the Soviet presence in Afghanistan, in [The Hague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hague "The Hague"), Netherlands, 1985[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/RIAN_archive_476785_Soviet_Army_soldiers_return_from_Afghanistan.jpg/500px-RIAN_archive_476785_Soviet_Army_soldiers_return_from_Afghanistan.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:RIAN_archive_476785_Soviet_Army_soldiers_return_from_Afghanistan.jpg) Soviet soldiers return from Afghanistan, October 1986[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/%D0%A0%D0%B0%D0%B7%D0%B2%D0%B5%D0%B4%D1%80%D0%BE%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%BD%D0%B0_%D1%80%D0%B5%D0%B9%D0%B4%D0%B5.jpg/500px-%D0%A0%D0%B0%D0%B7%D0%B2%D0%B5%D0%B4%D1%80%D0%BE%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%BD%D0%B0_%D1%80%D0%B5%D0%B9%D0%B4%D0%B5.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:%D0%A0%D0%B0%D0%B7%D0%B2%D0%B5%D0%B4%D1%80%D0%BE%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%BD%D0%B0_%D1%80%D0%B5%D0%B9%D0%B4%D0%B5.jpg) [Spetsnaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spetsnaz "Spetsnaz") troops interrogate a captured [mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujahideen "Mujahideen") with an RPG, rounds and AK-47 in the background, 1986

Between 25 December 1979, and 15 February 1989, 620,000 soldiers served with the forces in Afghanistan (though there were only 80,000–104,000 serving at one time): 525,000 in the Army, 90,000 with border troops and other [KGB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KGB "KGB") sub-units, 5,000 in formations of [MVD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Internal_Affairs_(Soviet_Union) "Ministry of Internal Affairs (Soviet Union)") [Internal Troops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_Troops_of_the_Soviet_Union "Internal Troops of the Soviet Union"), and police forces.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\] A further 21,000 personnel were with the Soviet troop contingent over the same period doing various white collar and blue-collar jobs.

The total official fatalities of the Soviet Armed Forces, Border Troops, and Internal Troops came to 14,453.[&#8202;](#ref-back-vfw.org-6)[\[18\]](#refnote-vfw.org) Soviet Army formations, units, and HQ elements lost 13,833, KGB sub-units lost 572, MVD formations lost 28, and other ministries and departments lost 20 men. During this period 312 servicemen were missing in action or taken prisoner; 119 were later freed, of whom 97 returned to the USSR and 22 went to other countries. In reports made by the Russian General Staff, personnel of the 40th Army killed in the war exceeded 26,000.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SovietWar2002-0)[\[249\]](#refnote-SovietWar2002):43–44

Of the troops deployed, 53,753 were wounded, injured, or sustained concussions and 415,932 fell sick. A high proportion of casualties were those who fell ill. This was because of local climatic and sanitary conditions, which were such that acute infections spread rapidly among the troops. There were 115,308 cases of infectious [hepatitis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hepatitis "Hepatitis"), 31,080 of [typhoid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Typhoid "Typhoid") fever, and 140,665 of other diseases. Of the 11,654 who were discharged from the army after being wounded, maimed, or contracting serious diseases, 10,751 men were left disabled.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Krivosheev1993-1)[\[12\]](#refnote-Krivosheev1993)

Material losses were as follows:[&#8202;](#ref-back-vfw.org-7)[\[18\]](#refnote-vfw.org)

- [451 aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Soviet_aircraft_crashes_in_Afghanistan "List of Soviet aircraft crashes in Afghanistan") (includes 333 [helicopters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helicopters "Helicopters"))
- 147 [tanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tank "Tank")
- 1,314 [IFV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IFV "IFV")/ [APCs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armoured_personnel_carrier "Armoured personnel carrier")
- 433 [artillery guns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artillery "Artillery") and [mortars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mortar_(weapon) "Mortar (weapon)")
- 11,369 cargo and fuel tanker trucks.

In early 1987 a CIA report estimated that, from 1979 to 1986, the Soviet military spent 18 billion rubles on the war in Afghanistan (not counting other costs incurred to the Soviet state such as economic and military aid to the DRA). The CIA noted that this was the equivalent of US$50 billion[&#8202;](#ref-back-252-0)[\[250\]](#refnote-252) ($115 billion in 2019 USD).[&#8202;](#ref-back-253-0)[\[251\]](#refnote-253) The report credited the relatively low cost to the small size of the Soviet deployment and the fact that the supply lines to Afghanistan were very short (in some cases, easier and cheaper than internal USSR lines). Military aid to the DRA's armed forces totaled 9.124 billion rubles from 1980 to 1989 (peaking at 3.972 billion rubles in 1989).[&#8202;](#ref-back-MinkovSmolynec-0)[\[252\]](#refnote-MinkovSmolynec):4 Financial and economic aid were also significant; by 1990, 75 percent of the Afghan state's income came from Soviet aid.[&#8202;](#ref-back-MinkovSmolynec-1)[\[252\]](#refnote-MinkovSmolynec):17

### Casualties and destruction in Afghanistan

Main article: [Atrocity crimes in the Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atrocity_crimes_in_the_Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War "Atrocity crimes in the Soviet–Afghan War")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/%D0%9F%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%86%D1%96%D0%B2%D0%BD%D0%B8%D0%BA%D0%B8_%D0%9C%D0%9A%D0%A7%D0%A5_%D0%B4%D0%BE%D0%BF%D0%BE%D0%BC%D0%B0%D0%B3%D0%B0%D1%8E%D1%82%D1%8C_%D0%B4%D1%96%D1%82%D1%8F%D0%BC%2C_%D1%89%D0%BE_%D0%BF%D0%BE%D1%81%D1%82%D1%80%D0%B0%D0%B6%D0%B4%D0%B0%D0%BB%D0%B8_%D1%87%D0%B5%D1%80%D0%B5%D0%B7_%D0%BC%D1%96%D0%BD%D0%BD%D1%83_%D0%BD%D0%B5%D0%B1%D0%B5%D0%B7%D0%BF%D0%B5%D0%BA%D1%83_%D0%B2_%D0%90%D1%84%D0%B3%D0%B0%D0%BD%D1%96%D1%81%D1%82%D0%B0%D0%BD%D1%96.jpg/500px-thumbnail.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:%D0%9F%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%86%D1%96%D0%B2%D0%BD%D0%B8%D0%BA%D0%B8_%D0%9C%D0%9A%D0%A7%D0%A5_%D0%B4%D0%BE%D0%BF%D0%BE%D0%BC%D0%B0%D0%B3%D0%B0%D1%8E%D1%82%D1%8C_%D0%B4%D1%96%D1%82%D1%8F%D0%BC,_%D1%89%D0%BE_%D0%BF%D0%BE%D1%81%D1%82%D1%80%D0%B0%D0%B6%D0%B4%D0%B0%D0%BB%D0%B8_%D1%87%D0%B5%D1%80%D0%B5%D0%B7_%D0%BC%D1%96%D0%BD%D0%BD%D1%83_%D0%BD%D0%B5%D0%B1%D0%B5%D0%B7%D0%BF%D0%B5%D0%BA%D1%83_%D0%B2_%D0%90%D1%84%D0%B3%D0%B0%D0%BD%D1%96%D1%81%D1%82%D0%B0%D0%BD%D1%96.jpg) A member of the [International Committee of the Red Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Committee_of_the_Red_Cross "International Committee of the Red Cross") helping a wounded Afghan child walk in 1986

The war resulted in the deaths of approximately 3,000,000 Afghans, civilian death and destruction from the war was massive and detrimental.[&#8202;](#ref-back-255-0)[\[253\]](#refnote-255) Estimates of Afghan civilian deaths vary from 562,000 to 2,000,000.[&#8202;](#ref-back-562k-2)[\[27\]](#refnote-562k)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEKlass2018129-2)[\[29\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEKlass2018129)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGoodson20115-2)[\[30\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGoodson20115)

Afghan mujahideen losses were also high. According to preliminary Western estimates, the resistance lost 90,000 fighters killed and the same number wounded.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGiustozzi2000-0)[\[254\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGiustozzi2000)\[ _[pageneeded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources "Wikipedia:Citing sources")_\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Russische_Schmetterlingsmine_PFM-1.jpg/500px-Russische_Schmetterlingsmine_PFM-1.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Russische_Schmetterlingsmine_PFM-1.jpg) A [PFM-1 mine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PFM-1_mine "PFM-1 mine"), often mistaken for a toy by children. The mine's shape was dictated by aerodynamics.[&#8202;](#ref-back-257-0)[\[255\]](#refnote-257)

The [Geneva Accords of 1988](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Accords_(1988) "Geneva Accords (1988)"), which ultimately led to the withdrawal of the Soviet forces in early 1989, left the Afghan government in ruins. The accords had failed to address adequately the issue of the post-occupation period and the future governance of Afghanistan. The assumption among most Western diplomats was that the Soviet-backed government in Kabul would soon collapse; however, this was not to happen for another three years. During this time the Interim Islamic Government of Afghanistan (IIGA) was established in exile. The exclusion of key groups such as refugees and [Shias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shia "Shia"), combined with major disagreements between the different Mujahideen factions, meant that the IIGA never succeeded in acting as a functional government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Barakat2004-0)[\[256\]](#refnote-Barakat2004):5

Before the war, Afghanistan was already one of the world's poorest countries. The prolonged conflict left Afghanistan ranked 170 out of 174 in the UNDP's _Human Development Index_, making Afghanistan one of the least developed countries in the world.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Barakat2004-1)[\[256\]](#refnote-Barakat2004):7

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a0/AfghanGuerillainUS1986e.JPEG/500px-AfghanGuerillainUS1986e.JPEG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:AfghanGuerillainUS1986e.JPEG) Afghan guerrillas that were chosen to receive medical treatment in the United States, [Norton Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norton_Air_Force_Base "Norton Air Force Base"), California, 1986

Once the Soviets withdrew, US interest in Afghanistan slowly decreased over the following four years, much of it administered through the DoD Office of Humanitarian Assistance, under the then Director of HA, George M. Dykes III. With the first years of the Clinton Administration in Washington, DC, all aid ceased. The US decided not to help with reconstruction of the country, instead handing the interests of the country over to US allies Saudi Arabia and Pakistan. Pakistan quickly took advantage of this opportunity and forged relations with [warlords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warlord "Warlord") and later the [Taliban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliban "Taliban"), to secure trade interests and routes. The ten years following the war saw much ecological and agrarian destruction—from wiping out the country's trees through logging practices, which has destroyed all but 2% of forest cover country-wide, to substantial uprooting of wild [pistachio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pistachio "Pistachio") trees for the exportation of their roots for therapeutic uses, to [opium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opium "Opium") agriculture.[&#8202;](#ref-back-259-0)[\[257\]](#refnote-259)

Captain Tarlan Eyvazov, a soldier in the Soviet forces during the war, stated that the Afghan children's future is destined for war. Eyvazov said, "Children born in Afghanistan at the start of the war... have been brought up in war conditions, this is their way of life." Eyvazov's theory was later strengthened when the Taliban movement developed and formed from orphans or refugee children who were forced by the Soviets to flee their homes and relocate their lives in Pakistan. The swift rise to power, from the young Taliban in 1996, was the result of the disorder and civil war that had warlords running wild because of the complete breakdown of law and order in Afghanistan after the departure of the Soviets.[&#8202;](#ref-back-260-0)[\[258\]](#refnote-260)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bhutta-0)[\[259\]](#refnote-Bhutta)

The _CIA World Fact Book_ reported that as of 2004, Afghanistan still owed $8 billion in bilateral debt, mostly to Russia,[&#8202;](#ref-back-262-0)[\[260\]](#refnote-262) however, in 2007 Russia agreed to cancel most of the debt.[&#8202;](#ref-back-263-0)[\[261\]](#refnote-263)

### Refugees

Main articles: [Afghan refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_refugees "Afghan refugees") and [Afghans in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghans_in_Pakistan "Afghans in Pakistan")

A total of 5.5 million Afghans were made [refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugee "Refugee") by the war—a full one third of the country's pre-war population—fleeing the country to Pakistan or Iran.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEKaplan200811-0)[\[262\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEKaplan200811) Another estimate states 6.2 million refugees.[&#8202;](#ref-back-265-0)[\[263\]](#refnote-265) By the end of 1981, the [UN High Commission for Refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_High_Commission_for_Refugees "UN High Commission for Refugees") reported that Afghans represented the largest group of refugees in the world.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sliwinski-0)[\[264\]](#refnote-Sliwinski)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEKlass2018129-3)[\[29\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEKlass2018129)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGoodson20115-3)[\[30\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGoodson20115)

Of the Afghan refugees, 3.3 million were housed in Pakistan by 1988, some of whom continue to live in the country. Of this total, about 100,000 were based in the city of [Peshawar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peshawar "Peshawar"), while more than two million were located in other parts of the northwestern province of [Khyber Pakhtunkhwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khyber_Pakhtunkhwa "Khyber Pakhtunkhwa") (then known as the North-West Frontier Province).[&#8202;](#ref-back-267-0)[\[265\]](#refnote-267)[&#8202;](#ref-back-nation.com.pk-0)[\[266\]](#refnote-nation.com.pk) At the same time, close to two million [Afghans were living in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghans_in_Iran "Afghans in Iran"). Over the years Pakistan and [Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran "Iran") have imposed tighter controls on refugees which have resulted in numerous returnees.[&#8202;](#ref-back-269-0)[\[267\]](#refnote-269)[&#8202;](#ref-back-270-0)[\[268\]](#refnote-270) In 2012 Pakistan banned extensions of visas to foreigners.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nation.com.pk-1)[\[266\]](#refnote-nation.com.pk)[&#8202;](#ref-back-271-0)[\[269\]](#refnote-271) Afghan refugees have also settled in India and became Indian citizens over time.[&#8202;](#ref-back-272-0)[\[270\]](#refnote-272)[&#8202;](#ref-back-273-0)[\[271\]](#refnote-273)[&#8202;](#ref-back-274-0)[\[272\]](#refnote-274) Some also made their way into North America, the [European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union "European Union"), Australia, and other parts of the world.[&#8202;](#ref-back-275-0)[\[273\]](#refnote-275) The photo of [Sharbat Gula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharbat_Gula "Sharbat Gula") placed on _[National Geographic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Geographic_Magazine "National Geographic Magazine")_ cover in 1985 became a symbol both of the 1980s Afghan conflict and of the refugee situation.

**Estimated number of Afghan refugees by destination, as of 1984[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEAmstutz1994224-0)[\[274\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEAmstutz1994224)**

### Effect on Afghan society

The legacy of the war introduced a culture of guns, drugs and terrorism in Afghanistan. The traditional power structure was also changed in favor of the powerful Mujahideen militias:[&#8202;](#ref-back-cdlib-10)[\[131\]](#refnote-cdlib)

> "In present-day Afghanistan the groups of clergy, community elders, intelligentsia, and the military cannot be seen."[&#8202;](#ref-back-cdlib-11)[\[131\]](#refnote-cdlib)

The [militarization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Militarization "Militarization") transformed the society in the country, leading to heavily armed police, private bodyguards, and openly armed civil defense groups becoming the norm in Afghanistan both during the war and decades thereafter.[&#8202;](#ref-back-277-0)[\[275\]](#refnote-277)

The war also altered the ethnic balance of power in the country. While [Pashtuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtuns "Pashtuns") were historically politically dominant since the modern foundation of the [Durrani Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durrani_Empire "Durrani Empire") in 1747, many of the well-organized pro-Mujahideen or pro-government groups consisted of [Tajiks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tajiks "Tajiks"), [Uzbeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uzbeks "Uzbeks") and [Hazaras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazaras "Hazaras"). With Pashtuns increasingly politically fragmented, their influence on the state was challenged.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Noor-2)[\[106\]](#refnote-Noor)

## Aftermath

Main article: [Consequences and legacy of the Soviet-Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consequences_and_legacy_of_the_Soviet-Afghan_War "Consequences and legacy of the Soviet-Afghan War")

Sources consistently characterize the Soviet–Afghan War as a political defeat for the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union "Soviet Union"), with some sources also defining the war as a military defeat for the Soviets.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEPlügge2023340-0)[\[276\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEPlügge2023340)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTETreninMalashenko20107-0)[\[277\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTETreninMalashenko20107) Modern literature is divided, with other sources also calling the war a political defeat for the Soviets, but a military draw.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SovietWar2002-1)[\[249\]](#refnote-SovietWar2002):XXV (editors' preface)

The war and Afghanistan has typically been referred to as "Soviet [Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_War "Vietnam War")", but despite modern analogy, the result is that Afghanistan was much worse than a Soviet Vietnam, as the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_of_America "United States of America") had survived Vietnam, while the Soviets did not survive Afghanistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-280-0)[\[278\]](#refnote-280)

## Media and popular culture

Main article: [Soviet–Afghan War in popular culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War_in_popular_culture "Soviet–Afghan War in popular culture")

Within Afghanistan, [war rugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_rugs "War rugs") were a popular form of carpet designs woven by victims of the war.

## Perception in Afghanistan

Main article: [Consequences and legacy of the Soviet-Afghan War §Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consequences_and_legacy_of_the_Soviet-Afghan_War#Afghanistan "Consequences and legacy of the Soviet-Afghan War")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/AfghanAirForce1.jpg/500px-AfghanAirForce1.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:AfghanAirForce1.jpg) Afghans commemorating [Mujahideen Victory Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujahideen_Victory_Day "Mujahideen Victory Day") in Kabul (2007)

The war has left a controversial legacy for Afghan people.[&#8202;](#ref-back-281-0)[\[279\]](#refnote-281) Many Afghans see this as a victory over the Soviets and a source of pride.[&#8202;](#ref-back-282-0)[\[280\]](#refnote-282)[&#8202;](#ref-back-283-0)[\[281\]](#refnote-283) The [Mujahideen Victory Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujahideen_Victory_Day "Mujahideen Victory Day") is an annual holiday in Afghanistan on 28 April, to commemorate the subsequent overthrow of the Soviet-backed regime in 1992. However, it is a controversial event to Afghans. Some Afghans honor the fighters and sacrifice made by the Mujahideen to defeat a major power, but others view the victory as a prelude to the brutal [1990s civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Civil_War_(1992%E2%80%9396) "Afghan Civil War (1992–96)") that divided the country politically and ethnically.[&#8202;](#ref-back-PRI-0)[\[282\]](#refnote-PRI)

## Perception in the former Soviet Union

Main article: [Consequences and legacy of the Soviet-Afghan War §Legacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consequences_and_legacy_of_the_Soviet-Afghan_War#Legacy "Consequences and legacy of the Soviet-Afghan War")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1d/RIAN_archive_21116_Afghan_war_veterans_meeting.jpg/500px-RIAN_archive_21116_Afghan_war_veterans_meeting.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:RIAN_archive_21116_Afghan_war_veterans_meeting.jpg) A meeting of Russian war veterans from Afghanistan, 1990

The war left a long legacy in the former Soviet Union and following its collapse. Along with losses, it brought physical disabilities and widespread drug addiction throughout the USSR.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bel-0)[\[283\]](#refnote-Bel)

The remembrance of Soviet soldiers killed in Afghanistan and elsewhere internationally are commemorated annually on 15 February in Russia, Ukraine and Belarus. Veterans of the war are often referred to as афганцы, _Afgantsy_ in [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language "Russian language").[&#8202;](#ref-back-286-0)[\[284\]](#refnote-286)

## Notes

1. [&#8202;](#refnote-38)[↑](#ref-back-38-0)The Soviet military deployment had been variously described as an " [invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion "Invasion")" (by the [Western world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_world "Western world") and the Afghan rebels) or as an " [intervention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intervention_(international_law) "Intervention (international law)")" (by the [Eastern Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc "Eastern Bloc") and the Afghan government).[&#8202;](#ref-back-35-0)[\[34\]](#refnote-35)[&#8202;](#ref-back-36-0)[\[35\]](#refnote-36) It was described as a hostile invasion by [Amnesty International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amnesty_International "Amnesty International").[&#8202;](#ref-back-37-0)[\[36\]](#refnote-37)

## Notes

1. [&#8202;](#refnote-2)[↑](#ref-back-2-0)see [Afghanistan–Pakistan border skirmishes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93Pakistan_border_skirmishes "Afghanistan–Pakistan border skirmishes") for the Pakistani involvement in the border war

     - De La Pedraja, René (21 November 2018). [_The Russian Military Resurgence: Post-Soviet Decline and Rebuilding, 1992–2018_](https://books.google.com/books?id=SYd7DwAAQBAJ&pg=PA36). For bin Laden, the mujahideen—a vanguard army of true Muslims fighting for the construction of a legitimate Islamic State—had prevailed against a much stronger non-Muslim power presaged the Islamic revolution. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_(identifier) "Doi (identifier)"):[10.1080/19436149.2025.2499294](https://doi.org/10.1080%2F19436149.2025.2499294). [ISSN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISSN_(identifier) "ISSN (identifier)") [1943-6149](https://search.worldcat.org/issn/1943-6149).
- Shaw, Tamsin, " [Ethical Espionage](https://www.nybooks.com/articles/2024/02/08/ethical-espionage-spies-calder-walton/)" (review of [Calder Walton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calder_Walton "Calder Walton"), _Spies: The Epic Intelligence War Between East and West_, Simon and Schuster, 2023, 672 pp.; and [Cécile Fabre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%A9cile_Fabre "Cécile Fabre"), _Spying Through a Glass Darkly: The Ethics of Espionage and Counter-Intelligence_, Oxford University Press, 251 pp., 2024), _[The New York Review of Books](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Review_of_Books "The New York Review of Books")_, vol. LXXI, no. 2 (8 February 2024), pp.32, 34–35. "\[I\]n Walton's view, there was scarcely a US [covert action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covert_operation "Covert operation") that was a long-term strategic success, with the possible exception of intervention in the Soviet-Afghan War (a disastrous military fiasco for the [Soviets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union "Soviet Union")) and perhaps support for the anti-Soviet [Solidarity movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solidarity_(Polish_trade_union) "Solidarity (Polish trade union)") in [Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poland "Poland")."
